```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related-work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Neurosymbolic Reasoning with LLMs.}
5	Prompting-based pipelines couple LLMs with external solvers for logical reasoning.
6	SatLM~\cite{satlm} generates SAT formulas, Logic-LM~\cite{logic-lm} translates to first-order logic, LINC~\cite{linc} generates logic programs, and LoRP~\cite{lorp} uses Prolog as a reasoning backend; FOVER~\cite{fover-pei2025} adds iterative verification.
7	All evaluate on execution accuracy alone, without diagnosing structural quality of formalizations.
8	Recent RL approaches train LLMs against solver feedback~\cite{mellgren-prolog-grpo, logic-rl, thought-like-pro}, optimizing prove rate with binary execution reward.
9	STA differs in diagnostic focus: ISR exposes that execution accuracy conflates faithful derivation with degenerate shortcuts, and our finding that 87\% of proved outputs under exec-only GRPO embed the conclusion in the premises (\S\ref{sec:diagnostic}) suggests prior prove-rate gains may overstate genuine reasoning improvement.
10	We adopt FOLIO~\cite{folio} as it is the only publicly available NLI benchmark providing both FOL and Prolog annotations, enabling our cross-logic diagnostic transfer experiments; ProofWriter~\cite{proofwriter} and ProntoQA~\cite{prontoqa} offer synthetic logical reasoning benchmarks but lack the multi-logic annotation required for cross-logic comparison.
11	
12	\paragraph{Reward Shaping and Process Supervision.}
13	Binary outcome rewards motivate process-level supervision: learned process reward models~\cite[PRMs;][]{lightman-verify-step, math-shepherd} provide step-level feedback, solver-grounded signals~\cite{rlsf} leverage formal verification, and rubric-based rewards~\cite{rubric-grpo} encode structured evaluation criteria.
14	However, process rewards introduce their own failure modes: design choices dominate signal effects~\cite{gao-effective-rl-reward}, process and outcome rewards can conflict~\cite{prof-process-outcome}, and PRMs degrade at distribution shift~\cite{qwen-prm-lessons, processbench, progrs-outcome-conditioned}.
15	Recent PRM advances address these limitations through generative verification chains~\cite{thinkprm}, multi-domain training data~\cite{versaprm}, and reasoning-driven evaluation~\cite{rprm}, but remain learned and require curated supervision.
16	ISR is solver-grounded rather than learned (no reward-model noise), yet our \S\ref{sec:goodhart} result shows that even non-learned, deterministic process signals Goodhart when optimized---suggesting the problem is fundamental to process-level reward shaping, not specific to learned models.
17	
18	\paragraph{Reward Hacking and Goodhart's Law.}
19	Prior work establishes Goodhart's Law theoretically~\cite{manheim-goodhart-taxonomy, skalse-reward-hacking, karwowski-goodhart-rl, pan-reward-misspecification, kwa-catastrophic-goodhart} and observes overoptimization scaling in RLHF~\cite{gao-reward-overoptimization}; recent empirical reports document frontier models reward-hacking code-generation tasks in practice~\cite{metr-reward-hacking, macdiarmid-emergent-misalignment}, with learned reward hacking generalizing to misaligned behaviors in unrelated contexts~\cite{school-reward-hacks}.
20	In LLMs, specification gaming~\cite{bondarenko-spec-gaming}, formalization gaming~\cite{kim-game-formalization}, and spurious RLVR rewards~\cite{spurious-rewards} have been documented, but the proxy in these analyses is a learned reward model.
21	Kim et al.~\cite{kim-game-formalization} examine formalization gaming in Lean 4 theorem proving, identifying axiom fabrication and premise mistranslation as unfaithfulness modes via inference-time prompting on FOLIO and Multi-LogiEval.
22	Our work is complementary: while Kim et al.\ diagnose gaming in inference-time outputs of frontier models, we study gaming that \emph{emerges during RL training} and provide ISR as a structural diagnostic with a systematic seven-mode taxonomy and cross-logic transfer analysis.
23	Our setting differs in two respects: ISR is deterministic and solver-derived, eliminating reward-model noise; and the Goodhart effect operates at a \emph{meta} level---ISR was designed to detect gaming of execution reward, yet itself becomes gamed when optimized.
24	This meta-level phenomenon---where a diagnostic for gaming becomes the target of gaming---has not been previously documented in the reward hacking literature.
25	
26	\paragraph{Mutation Testing.}
27	STA adapts the leave-one-out mutation paradigm~\cite{demillo1978, offutt-coupling, jia-harman-survey, just-mutants-real-faults} from software testing to neurosymbolic formalizations: the ``program'' is the solver input, the ``test'' is proof success, and the diagnostic target is component necessity for proof validity.
28	While mutation testing has been applied to neural networks~\cite{llmorpheus} and test suite adequacy, our application to \emph{solver inputs} for diagnosing structural quality of model-generated formalizations is novel.
29	The novelty lies in application and findings (ISR collapse, meta-Goodhart, hacking taxonomy), not in the perturbation technique itself.
30	
```

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\begin{figure*}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=0.92\textwidth]{figures/fig1_sta_overview.pdf}
7	\caption{Solver Trace Ablation (STA). (a) Formalization pipeline: NL is formalized and verified by an external solver. (b) STA protocol: leave-one-out ablation on proved formalizations yields ISR. (c) ISR as diagnostic vs.\ as reward: when the diagnostic becomes the target, meta-level Goodhart's Law emerges.}
8	\label{fig:sta-overview}
9	\end{figure*}
10	
11	\subsection{Task and GRPO Training}
12	\label{sec:task}
13	
14	Given natural language premises $P_1,\ldots,P_k$ and a conclusion $C$, the task is to produce a formal logic program $F=\{c_1,\ldots,c_n\}$, where each component $c_i$ is a premise (FOL) or a clause/fact (Prolog), with one distinguished component encoding the query.
15	An external solver $S$ returns $S(F)\in\{\textit{proved},\textit{failed}\}$; we use Prover9 for FOL and SWI-Prolog for Prolog (\autoref{fig:sta-overview}a).
16	
17	We train formalizers with GRPO~\citep{deepseekmath} using $K{=}8$ rollouts per prompt.
18	Each rollout receives a binary \emph{execution reward}
19	$r_{\text{exec}}(x) = \mathbf{1}[S(F_x) = \textit{proved}]$;
20	group-relative advantages are computed within each prompt's rollout group, and the policy update maximizes a clipped surrogate objective with KL regularization.
21	This setup optimizes prove rate but provides no signal about \emph{structural quality}: a formalization that embeds the conclusion among its premises receives the same reward as a faithful multi-step derivation.
22	
23	\subsection{Solver Trace Ablation (STA)}
24	\label{sec:sta}
25	
26	To diagnose structural quality, STA tests whether each component of a proved formalization is individually necessary for proof success (\autoref{fig:sta-overview}b).
27	Given a proved $x$ with $n$ components, STA constructs $n$ ablated variants $x\setminus\{c_i\}$, each removing one component, and re-runs the solver.
28	A component is \emph{necessary} if its removal causes proof failure: $S(x\setminus\{c_i\})=\textit{failed}$.
29	What constitutes a component depends on the logic type: individual premises in FOL, individual clauses or ground facts in Prolog.
30	
31	\paragraph{Intensional Structural Reward.}
32	ISR is the fraction of necessary components:
33	\begin{equation}
34	  \text{ISR}(x) = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\bigl[S(x\setminus\{c_i\})=\textit{failed}\bigr]
35	  \label{eq:isr}
36	\end{equation}
37	ISR $\in[0,1]$ is defined only for proved formalizations ($r_{\text{exec}}(x){=}1$): ISR${=}1$ indicates syntactic component necessity; ISR${=}0$ indicates complete redundancy.
38	The protocol is solver-agnostic, requiring $n{+}1$ solver calls per sample.
39	STA draws on the LOO intuition from mutation testing~\citep{demillo1978, jia-harman-survey} but operates on solver \emph{inputs} (formalizations) rather than program code, targeting component necessity rather than test adequacy.
40	Pseudocode is in Appendix~\ref{app:algorithm}.
41	
42	\paragraph{Limitations of ISR.}
43	ISR measures syntactic component necessity, not semantic faithfulness to the natural language input.
44	It is also subject to a component-count confound: formalizations with fewer components yield mechanically higher ISR, since each carries a larger share of the proof burden under LOO.
45	We control for this confound in our analysis (\S\ref{sec:goodhart}).
46	
47	\subsection{Reward Conditions}
48	\label{sec:rewards}
49	
50	We study six reward conditions (\autoref{fig:sta-overview}c).
51	For all RL conditions, bonuses apply only to proved samples; unproved samples receive $r{=}0$.
52	
53	\noindent\textbf{Exec-only:} $r=r_{\text{exec}}$ (baseline).
54	\noindent\textbf{Additive ISR:} $r=r_{\text{exec}}\cdot(1+\beta\,\text{ISR}(x))$ with $\beta\in\{0.1,0.3\}$, gating the structural bonus on proof success.
55	\noindent\textbf{Additive Random:} $r=r_{\text{exec}}\cdot(1+\beta\,u)$, $u\sim\mathcal{U}(0,1)$, $\beta\in\{0.1,0.3\}$: control isolating ISR-specific structural signal from variance/scaling effects. We draw from $\mathcal{U}(0,1)$ to match ISR's $[0,1]$ range while preserving the execution-gating structure; this provides a conservative lower bound on ISR-specific effects since any structured signal---even random---may regularize training.
56	\noindent\textbf{SFT baseline:} supervised fine-tuning on gold formalizations, testing whether observed hacking is RL-specific.
57	
58	Each RL condition is trained with seeds $\{42,43,44\}$ controlling initialization and stochasticity; all seeds share identical training data (\S\ref{sec:setup}).
59	
60	\subsection{Hacking Taxonomy}
61	\label{sec:taxonomy-method}
62	
63	Across conditions, seeds, and logic types, we identify seven hacking modes (H1, H7, tautological collapse, repetition collapse, complexity-without-utility, contradiction explosion, query-as-fact gaming; full definitions in \S\ref{sec:taxonomy} and Appendix~\ref{app:hacking-modes}).
64	Modes are identified by a two-stage procedure: strict normalized text matching catches H1/H7; functional analysis of the per-sample $n_{\text{necessary}}$ distribution from STA catches modes that evade text matching.
```

(a) Formalization pipeline: NL is formalized and verified by an external solver. (b) STA protocol: leave-one-out ablation on proved formalizations yields ISR. (c) ISR as diagnostic vs.\\ as reward: when the diagnostic becomes the target, meta-level Goodhart's Law emerges.}\n\\label{fig:sta-overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Task and GRPO Training}\n\\label{sec:task}\n\nGiven natural language premises $P_1,\\ldots,P_k$ and a conclusion $C$, the task is to produce a formal logic program $F=\\{c_1,\\ldots,c_n\\}$, where each component $c_i$ is a premise (FOL) or a clause/fact (Prolog), with one distinguished component encoding the query.\nAn external solver $S$ returns $S(F)\\in\\{\\textit{proved},\\textit{failed}\\}$; we use Prover9 for FOL and SWI-Prolog for Prolog (\\autoref{fig:sta-overview}a).\n\nWe train formalizers with GRPO~\\citep{deepseekmath} using $K{=}8$ rollouts per prompt.\nEach rollout receives a binary \\emph{execution reward}\n$r_{\\text{exec}}(x) = \\mathbf{1}[S(F_x) = \\textit{proved}]$;\ngroup-relative advantages are computed within each prompt's rollout group, and the policy update maximizes a clipped surrogate objective with KL regularization.\nThis setup optimizes prove rate but provides no signal about \\emph{structural quality}: a formalization that embeds the conclusion among its premises receives the same reward as a faithful multi-step derivation.\n\n\\subsection{Solver Trace Ablation (STA)}\n\\label{sec:sta}\n\nTo diagnose structural quality, STA tests whether each component of a proved formalization is individually necessary for proof success (\\autoref{fig:sta-overview}b).\nGiven a proved $x$ with $n$ components, STA constructs $n$ ablated variants $x\\setminus\\{c_i\\}$, each removing one component, and re-runs the solver.\nA component is \\emph{necessary} if its removal causes proof failure: $S(x\\setminus\\{c_i\\})=\\textit{failed}$.\nWhat constitutes a component depends on the logic type: individual premises in FOL, individual clauses or ground facts in Prolog.\n\n\\paragraph{Intensional Structural Reward.}\nISR is the fraction of necessary components:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\text{ISR}(x) = \\frac{1}{n}\\sum_{i=1}^{n}\\mathbf{1}\\bigl[S(x\\setminus\\{c_i\\})=\\textit{failed}\\bigr]\n  \\label{eq:isr}\n\\end{equation}\nISR $\\in[0,1]$ is defined only for proved formalizations ($r_{\\text{exec}}(x){=}1$): ISR${=}1$ indicates syntactic component necessity; ISR${=}0$ indicates complete redundancy.\nThe protocol is solver-agnostic, requiring $n{+}1$ solver calls per sample.\nSTA draws on the LOO intuition from mutation testing~\\citep{demillo1978, jia-harman-survey} but operates on solver \\emph{inputs} (formalizations) rather than program code, targeting component necessity rather than test adequacy.\nPseudocode is in Appendix~\\ref{app:algorithm}.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations of ISR.}\nISR measures syntactic component necessity, not semantic faithfulness to the natural language input.\nIt is also subject to a component-count confound: formalizations with fewer components yield mechanically higher ISR, since each carries a larger share of the proof burden under LOO.\nWe control for this confound in our analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}).\n\n\\subsection{Reward Conditions}\n\\label{sec:rewards}\n\nWe study six reward conditions (\\autoref{fig:sta-overview}c).\nFor all RL conditions, bonuses apply only to proved samples; unproved samples receive $r{=}0$.\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Exec-only:} $r=r_{\\text{exec}}$ (baseline).\n\\noindent\\textbf{Additive ISR:} $r=r_{\\text{exec}}\\cdot(1+\\beta\\,\\text{ISR}(x))$ with $\\beta\\in\\{0.1,0.3\\}$, gating the structural bonus on proof success.\n\\noindent\\textbf{Additive Random:} $r=r_{\\text{exec}}\\cdot(1+\\beta\\,u)$, $u\\sim\\mathcal{U}(0,1)$, $\\beta\\in\\{0.1,0.3\\}$: control isolating ISR-specific structural signal from variance/scaling effects. We draw from $\\mathcal{U}(0,1)$ to match ISR's $[0,1]$ range while preserving the execution-gating structure; this provides a conservative lower bound on ISR-specific effects since any structured signal---even random---may regularize training.\n\\noindent\\textbf{SFT baseline:} supervised fine-tuning on gold formalizations, testing whether observed hacking is RL-specific.\n\nEach RL condition is trained with seeds $\\{42,43,44\\}$ controlling initialization and stochasticity; all seeds share identical training data (\\S\\ref{sec:setup}).\n\n\\subsection{Hacking Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy-method}\n\nAcross conditions, seeds, and logic types, we identify seven hacking modes (H1, H7, tautological collapse, repetition collapse, complexity-without-utility, contradiction explosion, query-as-fact gaming; full definitions in \\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy} and Appendix~\\ref{app:hacking-modes}).\nModes are identified by a two-stage procedure: strict normalized text matching catches H1/H7; functional analysis of the per-sample $n_{\\text{necessary}}$ distribution from STA catches modes that evade text matching.",
      "numLines": 64,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 64
    }
  }
}

1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We evaluate STA along three axes: as a diagnostic for reward hacking (\S\ref{sec:diagnostic}); as a training reward exhibiting meta-level Goodhart's Law (\S\ref{sec:goodhart}); and as a cross-logic protocol (\S\ref{sec:crosslogic}). We synthesize results into a hacking taxonomy (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy}).
5	
6	\subsection{Setup}
7	\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	We train Qwen3-4B with GRPO~\citep{deepseekmath} using $K{=}8$ rollouts per prompt for 500 steps (default) or 1000 where noted, across seeds $\{42,43,44\}$.
10	Training data is a fixed 500-example subset of FOLIO~\citep{folio} (\texttt{train\_500\_seed42.jsonl}), held constant across all seeds and conditions. %% data: C001
11	We use Prover9 for FOL and SWI-Prolog 8.4.2 for Prolog.
12	Evaluation is on 100 held-out FOLIO examples with greedy decoding; all cross-run ISR values are computed by \texttt{eval\_sta.py} which applies STA uniformly. %% C004
13	We report prove rate (PR), proved-only ISR (ISR$^*$, over proved samples only), hacking mode percentages (H7, H1\footnote{Hacking mode numbering follows our taxonomy order (Appendix~\ref{app:hacking-modes}); H1 is the most prevalent mode.}), non-hacking rate\footnote{We define ``non-hacking'' as outputs not matching any of our seven identified hacking patterns (Table~\ref{tab:hacking-taxonomy-app}); this does not imply semantic correctness.}, and mean component count.
14	Six reward conditions are compared: \textbf{Exec-only}, \textbf{ISR~$\beta{\in}\{0.1,0.3\}$}, \textbf{Random~$\beta{\in}\{0.1,0.3\}$}, and \textbf{SFT}.
15	Outputs are classified into H7, H1, and Non-hacking using text matching cross-validated with functional LOO analysis.
16	Full hyperparameters are in Appendix~\ref{app:setup}.
17	
18	\subsection{ISR as Diagnostic}
19	\label{sec:diagnostic}
20	
21	\begin{figure}[t]
22	\centering
23	\includegraphics[width=0.95\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_isr_collapse.pdf}
24	\caption{ISR collapse under exec-only GRPO (seed~42). PR rises 32\%$\to$96\% while proved-only ISR falls 0.642$\to$0.003 over 1000 steps. By step 1000, 94.8\% of proved samples have ISR${=}0$ and mean component count inflates 6.58$\to$18.55 (contradiction explosion).}
25	\label{fig:isr-collapse}
26	\end{figure}
27	
28	\textbf{Collapse trajectory.}
29	Figure~\ref{fig:isr-collapse} traces ISR and PR over training for exec-only GRPO (seed~42).
30	At 100 steps: PR${=}32$\%, ISR$^*{=}0.642$. %% registry: plan003-eval-exec-only-r5
31	By 500 steps: PR${=}93$\% but ISR$^*{=}0.132$---87.1\% of proved samples embed the conclusion among the premises (H1). %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s42-r2
32	At 1000 steps: PR${=}96$\% while ISR$^*$ drops to 0.003 (H1${=}100$\%, mean components 18.55). %% registry: plan016-extended-1000-s42
33	The ISR$^*$ trajectory ($0.642{\to}0.132{\to}0.003$) is monotonically decreasing: extended training worsens structural degeneration, invisible to PR.
34	
35	\textbf{Cross-seed instability.}
36	The three exec-only seeds diverge radically: s42 hacks via H1 (PR${=}93$\%, H1${=}87.1$\%); s43 collapses to PR${=}13$\% (ISR$^*{=}0$) via repetition---87\% of outputs hit the max token length; s44 reaches PR${=}98$\% via tautological collapse (H7${=}100$\%, mean components 2.04). %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s42-r2, plan001-exec-only-s43-r4, plan001-exec-only-s44
37	Despite PR$\in[13,98]$\%, all three seeds exhibit structural degeneration detectable by ISR.
38	The cross-seed PR $\sigma{=}\pm47$\,pp is itself a finding: aggregate metrics obscure qualitatively different failure modes (per-seed details in Table~\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}).
39	
40	\textbf{SFT baseline.}
41	A supervised baseline achieves PR${=}14$\% with ISR$^*{=}0.571$: 92.9\% (13/14) [66.1\%, 99.8\%] of proved samples are non-hacking, only 1 exhibits H1, and no H7 instances occur. %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4
42	The near-absence of hacking is consistent with hacking correlating with optimization aggressiveness rather than being driven purely by model capacity or data limitations.
43	We note that the SFT PR level (14\%) is substantially lower than RL conditions, and the low hacking rate may partly reflect fewer opportunities for reward exploitation rather than a purely training-method difference; we address this confound via a 100-step RL checkpoint analysis in Appendix~\ref{app:100step}.
44	
45	\subsection{Meta-Level Goodhart's Law}
46	\label{sec:goodhart}
47	
48	ISR-as-reward provides the first empirical demonstration of Goodhart's Law at the meta level~\citep{manheim-goodhart-taxonomy}: the diagnostic itself becomes the target of gaming when optimized.
49	
50	\begin{table}[t]
51	\centering
52	\small
53	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%

58	Base (0-shot) & --- & 12 & --- & --- & --- & --- \\ %% registry: sta-phase0-baseline

61	Exec-only$^\dagger$ & --- & 93 & 0.132 & 0 & 87 & 13 \\ %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s42-r2

63	Random & 0.1 & \textbf{85}{\scriptsize$\pm$6} & 0.086{\scriptsize$\pm$.04} & 0 & 81 & 19 \\ %% registry: plan018-additive-random-b01-s42/s43-r2/s44
64	\midrule
65	ISR & 0.3 & 18 & 0.143 & --- & --- & --- \\ %% registry: plan002-additive-b03-s42
66	Random & 0.3 & 48 & 0.094 & --- & --- & --- \\ %% registry: plan017-additive-random-s42
67	\bottomrule
68	\end{tabular}}
69	\caption{Goodhart landscape across reward conditions. 3-seed rows show mean$\pm$std (ISR/Random $\beta{=}0.1$); other rows are single seed. $^\dagger$Seed~42; see \S\ref{sec:diagnostic} for 3-seed divergence. $\ddagger$ISR$^*$ omitted: inflated by H7 gaming (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}). H7 and H1 rates are simple means across seeds. Best PR and N-H\% bolded per group.}
70	\label{tab:goodhart-landscape}
71	\end{table}
72	
73	\textbf{Low-dose ISR ($\beta{=}0.1$): ISR-specific gaming.}
74	Table~\ref{tab:goodhart-landscape} summarizes results.
75	At $\beta{=}0.1$, 2/3 ISR seeds (s43, s44) are dominated by H7---a single premise identical to the conclusion, trivially achieving both high PR and ISR.
76	Across the three ISR seeds, H7 accounts for 64.5\% of proved samples (149/231); all three random controls show \emph{zero} H7 (0/256), confirming H7 is exclusively induced by the ISR signal. %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42/s43/s44 vs plan018-additive-random-b01-s42/s43-r2/s44
77	Conclusion embedding (H1+H7) is GRPO-universal at 68--97\% across RL conditions: random produces only H1 (70.9--93.2\%), while ISR converts H1$\to$H7 without reducing the total (Figure~\ref{fig:hacking-composition}; per-seed: Appendix Table~\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}).
78	Random also achieves higher and more stable PR (85$\pm$6\% vs 77$\pm$14\%). %% registry: plan018 vs plan002 3-seed means
79	
80	\textbf{Premise-count confound.}
81	Seed~42 under ISR---the only seed not dominated by H7---shows higher raw ISR$^*$ (0.415 vs 0.131 for random). %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42 (ISR*=0.4154), plan018-additive-random-b01-s42 (ISR*=0.1314)
82	This is entirely a premise-minimization artifact: ISR-trained models produce shorter formalizations (mean 5.58 vs 8.46 components), and LOO is mathematically biased toward shorter inputs. %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42 (comp=5.58), plan018-additive-random-b01-s42 (comp=8.46)
83	Restricting to non-hacking samples with $\geq$3 premises: ISR non-hacking ISR$^*{=}0.258$ ($n{=}11$) vs random non-hacking ISR$^*{=}0.243$ ($n{=}21$)---a 1.06$\times$ ratio, no meaningful advantage. %% D051 premise-count-matched analysis
84	Of ISR s42's 25 nominally non-hacking samples, 15 have only 1--2 premises, where ISR$\geq0.5$ is a mathematical artifact.
85	
86	\textbf{High-dose ISR ($\beta{=}0.3$): classic Goodhart.}
87	At $\beta{=}0.3$, ISR causes PR to collapse to 18\%---a 5.2$\times$ degradation from exec-only---while the matched random control achieves PR${=}48$\%. %% registry: plan002-additive-b03-s42, plan017-additive-random-s42
88	The two doses reveal a complete Goodhart trajectory~\citep{karwowski-goodhart-rl, gao-reward-overoptimization} at the meta level: low-dose proxy gaming (ISR inflation via H7) followed by high-dose performance collapse, with the proxy being gamed itself a gaming detector.
89	Under the taxonomy of \citet{manheim-goodhart-taxonomy}, $\beta{=}0.3$ exemplifies extremal Goodhart while $\beta{=}0.1$ instantiates a meta-level variant.
90	
91	\subsection{Cross-Logic Diagnostic Transfer}
92	\label{sec:crosslogic}
93	
94	We replicated both experiments using SWI-Prolog 8.4.2 (full results: Appendix Table~\ref{tab:prolog-crosslogic}).
95	Prolog exec-only confirms diagnostic transfer (2/3 seeds with ISR$^*{\approx}0$ despite moderate-high PR); the low ISR reflects \emph{redundant coding} (repeated ground facts), not absent reasoning---contrasting the metric-gaming high ISR under Prolog ISR-reward. %% registry: plan006-prolog-exec-s42 (ISR*=0.037), plan006-prolog-exec-s43-r3 (ISR*=0.015), plan006-prolog-exec-s44 (ISR*=0.387, small-sample n=21); C006
96	ISR-as-reward yields universal Goodhart with 0\% non-hacking across 185 proved samples (stronger than the FOL ${\sim}16$\% residual), via three distinct mechanisms: s42 clean H7 tautologies (ISR$^*{=}1.000$); s43 uppercase pseudo-Prolog functionally equivalent to H7 ($n_{\text{nec}}{=}1$, 38/38); s44 query-as-fact gaming, converting NL to snake\_case atoms used as both fact and query (ISR$^*{=}0.912$, $n_{\text{nec}}{=}1$, 95/97). %% registry: plan006-prolog-sta-s42 (H7=100%, ISR*=1.0), plan006-prolog-sta-s43 (H1=97.4%, n_nec=1 38/38), plan006-prolog-sta-s44 (PR=0.97, ISR=0.885, h7_functional=97.9%)
97	The diversity of pathways under identical data and reward shows Goodhart here is a family of convergent exploits (Figure~\ref{fig:prolog-comparison}); the text-classifier bypass by s44 motivates functional cross-validation of automated diagnostics.
98	
99	\subsection{Hacking Taxonomy}
100	\label{sec:taxonomy}
101	
102	\begin{figure}[t]
103	\centering
104	\includegraphics[width=0.9\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_hacking_composition.pdf}
105	\caption{Hacking composition across reward conditions (proved samples). ISR converts H1 (conclusion embedding) into more extreme H7 (conclusion duplication) without reducing total hacking; SFT is near-zero hacking (92.9\% non-hacking).}
106	\label{fig:hacking-composition}
107	\end{figure}
108	
109	The seven modes (quantitative breakdown and definitions in Appendix~\ref{app:hacking-modes}, Table~\ref{tab:hacking-taxonomy-app}): \textbf{H1} (GRPO-universal, 68--97\%), \textbf{H7} (ISR-specific, 64.5\% under $\beta{=}0.1$ vs.\ 0\% random), \textbf{tautological} (exec-only s44, 94\%), \textbf{repetition} (exec-only s43, 87\% hit max length), \textbf{contradiction explosion} (mean 18.55 components at 1000 steps), \textbf{complexity-without-utility} (Random $\beta{=}0.1$, 3.8\%), \textbf{query-as-fact} (Prolog ISR s44, bypasses text classifiers). %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s44, plan001-exec-only-s43-r4, plan016-extended-1000-s42, plan006-prolog-sta-s44
110	The reward signal determines hacking \emph{composition} but not \emph{prevalence}: total conclusion-in-premise rates are comparable across ISR and random (${\sim}82$\% pooled), but ISR converts H1 into H7.
111	SFT remains the only condition with near-zero hacking (Figure~\ref{fig:hacking-composition}), consistent with hacking being RL-reward-specific. %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4

other rows are single seed. $^\\dagger$Seed~42; see \\S\\ref{sec:diagnostic} for 3-seed divergence. $\\ddagger$ISR$^*$ omitted: inflated by H7 gaming (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}). H7 and H1 rates are simple means across seeds. Best PR and N-H\\% bolded per group.}\n\\label{tab:goodhart-landscape}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\textbf{Low-dose ISR ($\\beta{=}0.1$): ISR-specific gaming.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:goodhart-landscape} summarizes results.\nAt $\\beta{=}0.1$, 2/3 ISR seeds (s43, s44) are dominated by H7---a single premise identical to the conclusion, trivially achieving both high PR and ISR.\nAcross the three ISR seeds, H7 accounts for 64.5\\% of proved samples (149/231); all three random controls show \\emph{zero} H7 (0/256), confirming H7 is exclusively induced by the ISR signal. %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42/s43/s44 vs plan018-additive-random-b01-s42/s43-r2/s44\nConclusion embedding (H1+H7) is GRPO-universal at 68--97\\% across RL conditions: random produces only H1 (70.9--93.2\\%), while ISR converts H1$\\to$H7 without reducing the total (Figure~\\ref{fig:hacking-composition}; per-seed: Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}).\nRandom also achieves higher and more stable PR (85$\\pm$6\\% vs 77$\\pm$14\\%). %% registry: plan018 vs plan002 3-seed means\n\n\\textbf{Premise-count confound.}\nSeed~42 under ISR---the only seed not dominated by H7---shows higher 1.06$\\times$ ratio, no meaningful advantage. %% D051 premise-count-matched analysis\nOf ISR s42's 25 nominally non-hacking samples, 15 have only 1--2 premises, where ISR$\\geq0.5$ is a mathematical artifact.\n\n\\textbf{High-dose ISR ($\\beta{=}0.3$): classic Goodhart.}\nAt $\\beta{=}0.3$, ISR causes PR to collapse to 18\\%---a 5.2$\\times$ degradation from exec-only---while the matched random control achieves PR${=}48$\\%. %% registry: plan002-additive-b03-s42, plan017-additive-random-s42\nThe two doses reveal a complete Goodhart trajectory~\\citep{karwowski-goodhart-rl, gao-reward-overoptimization} at the meta level: low-dose proxy gaming (ISR inflation via H7) followed by high-dose performance collapse, with the proxy being gamed itself a gaming detector.\nUnder the ```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}
2	
3	We introduced Solver Trace Ablation (STA), a component-level diagnostic for neurosymbolic formalizers that exposes structural degeneration invisible to execution-based evaluation.
4	Applied to GRPO-trained models, ISR reveals a collapse trajectory hidden from prove rate, confirmed as RL-specific by an SFT baseline with near-zero hacking (\S\ref{sec:diagnostic}).
5	When repurposed as a training signal, ISR induces meta-level Goodhart's Law: at low dose, the model games the diagnostic itself through conclusion duplication absent under matched controls; at high dose, prove rate collapses; the sole apparent ISR improvement reduces to noise after controlling for a premise-count confound (\S\ref{sec:goodhart}).
6	Both diagnostic utility and Goodhart vulnerability transfer to Prolog/SWI-Prolog: ISR-as-reward yields universal gaming across all seeds with zero non-hacking proofs, each via a distinct mechanism (\S\ref{sec:crosslogic}).
7	Across six reward conditions and two logic types, we catalog seven hacking modes, including query-as-fact gaming that bypasses text-match classifiers (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy}).
8	
9	\paragraph{Interpreting ISR.}
10	ISR values must always be read relative to the optimization objective: under exec-only Prolog training, \emph{low} ISR reflects redundant coding (repeated ground facts), not absence of reasoning; under ISR-as-reward, \emph{high} ISR reflects metric gaming.
11	The premise-count confound---fewer components mechanically inflate ISR under LOO---applies to any ablation-based diagnostic on variable-length outputs.
12	While we use leave-one-out ablation for computational tractability ($n{+}1$ solver calls), Shapley value decomposition could provide more nuanced attribution at higher cost.
13	
14	\paragraph{Meta-Level Goodhart.}
15	ISR was designed to \emph{detect} reward gaming of $r_{\text{exec}}$, yet incorporating it into the reward causes the model to game ISR itself---a regress where any diagnostic metric risks becoming a target when optimized.
16	The dose-response pattern ($\beta{=}0.1$: proxy gaming; $\beta{=}0.3$: performance collapse) maps onto the trajectory predicted by~\cite{gao-reward-overoptimization}, but at a meta level.
17	ISR---and structurally similar diagnostics---should be used exclusively as post-hoc evaluation tools, never as training rewards.
18	The fundamental asymmetry (reliable as observer, unreliable as optimizer) may generalize to other perturbation-based quality metrics.
19	An open direction is developing reward functions that are robust to the gaming modes we identify---for instance, combining ISR with adversarial premise perturbation or semantic similarity constraints.
20	Combining ISR with semantic verification, adaptive reward weighting that detects gaming signatures, and extending STA to broader logic types are further promising directions.
21	
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	A GRPO-trained formalizer achieves a 93\% prove rate on first-order logic problems---yet 87\% of its proved outputs embed the conclusion verbatim among the premises.
5	The solver succeeds not because the model has learned to reason, but because the formalization trivially entails any conclusion that already appears as a premise.
6	This structural shortcut is invisible to execution-based evaluation: prove rate registers success regardless of whether the proof reflects genuine multi-step derivation or degenerate circular embedding.
7	
8	Prove rate---the dominant evaluation metric for neurosymbolic formalizers---conflates faithful reasoning with degenerate shortcuts, a gap especially consequential under reinforcement learning, where the optimizer exploits any shortcut the reward fails to penalize~\cite{skalse-reward-hacking, reward-hacking-survey}.
9	Recent work applies GRPO~\cite{deepseekmath} to train LLMs that generate solver-verified formal programs~\cite{mellgren-prolog-grpo, logic-rl} using binary execution feedback as reward; this drives rapid PR gains but gives no gradient on structural quality.
10	Process reward models~\cite{lightman-verify-step} offer step-level supervision but require expensive annotations and do not transfer to arbitrary solver-backed settings.
11	No existing method provides an annotation-free diagnostic for structural integrity in neurosymbolic outputs---one adaptable in principle to any solver supporting component-level input manipulation (see \S\ref{sec:related-work} for full context).
12	
13	We address this gap with \emph{Solver Trace Ablation} (STA), a diagnostic protocol inspired by mutation testing~\cite{demillo1978} that applies component necessity testing---leave-one-out (LOO) ablation on solver inputs---to quantify structural integrity (\autoref{fig:sta-overview}).
14	For each proved formalization, STA removes each component individually and re-runs the solver; the fraction of components whose removal causes proof failure yields the \emph{Intensional Structural Reward} (ISR).
15	ISR${=}$1 indicates syntactic component necessity---every component is individually required for proof success; ISR${=}$0 indicates complete structural redundancy.
16	We emphasize that ISR captures structural rather than semantic integrity: a downstream NLI evaluation finds ISR and task accuracy uncorrelated ($\rho{=}{-}0.049$, $p{=}0.187$), confirming it measures properties orthogonal to semantic correctness.
17	Applied as a post-hoc diagnostic on a GRPO formalizer trained with FOLIO~\cite{folio} data, ISR reveals a structural collapse trajectory entirely invisible to prove rate: ISR falls from 0.642 at 100 steps to 0.132 at 500 steps to 0.003 at 1000 steps---a 99.5\% degradation---while prove rate simultaneously rises from 32\% to 96\%.
18	Three random seeds diverge radically (PR $\sigma{=}47$\,pp), each converging to a qualitatively different degeneration mode: conclusion embedding, repetition collapse, and tautological collapse.
19	A supervised fine-tuning (SFT) baseline achieves 92.9\% non-hacking proofs with near-zero hacking, confirming that the structural collapse is a consequence of RL reward optimization, not model capacity or data limitations.
20	
21	A natural question is whether ISR can serve as a training signal. The answer is negative: when ISR is added to the reward ($r = r_{\text{exec}} \cdot (1 + \beta \cdot \text{ISR})$), the model games the diagnostic itself---\emph{meta-level Goodhart's Law}~\cite{manheim-goodhart-taxonomy, karwowski-goodhart-rl}.
22	At $\beta{=}0.1$, 64.5\% of proved samples exhibit ISR-specific conclusion duplication (H7) vs.\ 0\% under matched random reward, and the sole apparent improvement reduces to 1.06$\times$ after controlling for a premise-count confound (\S\ref{sec:goodhart}); at $\beta{=}0.3$ PR collapses to 18\% vs.\ 48\% for random control~\cite{gao-reward-overoptimization}.
23	The effect transfers and intensifies in Prolog/SWI-Prolog: all three seeds gaming, 0\% non-hacking---universal Goodhart, each via a distinct mechanism.
24	
25	Across 6 reward conditions, 3 seeds, 2 logic types, and up to 1000 training steps, we make four contributions:
26	
27	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
28	\item We introduce STA and establish ISR as a diagnostic for hacking invisible to PR, with a complete collapse trajectory ($0.642{\to}0.003$) over 1000 GRPO steps; SFT (92.9\% non-hacking) confirms hacking is RL-specific.
29	\item We characterize meta-level Goodhart in neurosymbolic RL and identify the premise-count confound behind the only apparent ISR gain (1.06$\times$).
30	\item We show cross-logic transfer to Prolog/SWI-Prolog with universal Goodhart (3/3 seeds, 0\% non-hacking), indicating simpler formal semantics enable more complete exploitation.
31	\item We catalog 7 hacking modes including query-as-fact gaming that bypasses text-match classifiers, motivating functional cross-validation of diagnostics.
32	\end{enumerate}
33

the fraction of components whose removal causes proof failure yields the \\emph{Intensional Structural Reward} (ISR).\nISR${=}$1 indicates syntactic component necessity---every component is individually required for proof success; ISR${=}$0 indicates complete structural redundancy.\nWe emphasize that ISR captures structural rather than semantic integrity: a downstream NLI evaluation finds ISR and task accuracy uncorrelated ($\\rho{=}{-}0.049$, $p{=}0.187$), confirming it measures properties orthogonal to semantic correctness.\nApplied as a post-hoc diagnostic on a GRPO formalizer trained with FOLIO~\\cite{folio} data, ISR reveals a structural collapse trajectory entirely invisible to prove rate: ISR falls from 0.642 at 100 steps to 0.132 at 500 steps to 0.003 at 1000 steps---a 99.5\\% degradation---while prove rate simultaneously rises from 32\\% to 96\\%.\nThree random seeds diverge radically (PR $\\sigma{=}47$\\,pp), each converging to a qualitatively different degeneration mode: conclusion embedding, repetition collapse, and tautological collapse.\nA supervised fine-tuning (SFT) baseline achieves 92.9\\% non-hacking proofs with near-zero hacking, confirming that the structural collapse is a consequence of RL reward optimization, not model capacity or data limitations.\n\nA natural question is whether ISR can serve as a training signal. The answer is negative: when ISR is added to the reward ($r = r_{\\text{exec}} \\cdot (1 + \\beta \\cdot \\text{ISR})$), the model games the diagnostic itself---\\emph{meta-level Goodhart's Law}~\\cite{manheim-goodhart-taxonomy, karwowski-goodhart-rl}.\nAt $\\beta{=}0.1$, 64.5\\% of proved samples exhibit ISR-specific conclusion duplication (H7) vs.\\ 0\\% under matched random reward, and the sole apparent improvement reduces to 1.06$\\times$ after controlling for a premise-count confound (\\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}); at $\\beta{=}0.3$ PR collapses to 18\\% vs.\\ 48\\% for random control~\\cite{gao-reward-overoptimization}.\nThe effect transfers and intensifies in Prolog/SWI-Prolog: all three seeds gaming, 0\\% non-hacking---universal Goodhart, each via a distinct mechanism.\n\nAcross 6 reward conditions, 3 seeds, 2 logic types, and up to 1000 training steps, we make four contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item We introduce STA and establish ISR as a diagnostic for hacking invisible to PR, with a complete collapse trajectory ($0.642{\\to}0.003$) over 1000 GRPO steps; SFT (92.9\\% non-hacking) confirms hacking is RL-specific.\n\\item We characterize meta-level Goodhart in neurosymbolic RL and identify the premise-count confound behind the only apparent ISR gain (1.06$\\times$).\n\\item We show cross-logic transfer to Prolog/SWI-Prolog with universal Goodhart (3/3 seeds, 0\\% non-hacking), indicating simpler formal semantics enable more complete exploitation.\n\\item We catalog 7 hacking modes including query-as-fact gaming that bypasses text-match classifiers, motivating functional cross-validation of diagnostics.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
      "numLines": 33,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 33
    }
  }
}

2	\label{sec:limitations}
3

5	ISR quantifies syntactic component necessity, not semantic faithfulness: a downstream NLI evaluation finds ISR and task accuracy uncorrelated (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.049$, $p{=}0.187$; Appendix~\ref{app:extended-discussion}).

We use 8 rollouts per prompt, where each one gets a binary execution reward based on whether the solver successfully proves the formalization. The policy updates are computed with group-relative advantages within each rollout group and a clipped surrogate objective with KL regularization. The issue is that this approach only optimizes for whether something proves, not whether the formalization is structurally sound—a conclusion sneaked into the premises gets the same reward as a proper multi-step derivation. The protocol requires $n{+}1$ solver calls per sample; pseudocode is in Appendix~\ref{app:algorithm}. There's also a component-count confound where formalizations with fewer components mechanically score higher under leave-one-out, since each component bears more weight. I address this in the Goodhart analysis section.

For the reward conditions, I'm laying out six different setups to test, where RL bonuses only apply to successfully proved samples. I'm setting up the random reward baseline to isolate whether the structured signal from ISR actually helps or if any bonus mechanism—even noise—can regularize training. By sampling uniformly to match ISR's range while keeping the execution-gating, this gives me a conservative estimate of ISR-specific effects. I'm also running a supervised fine-tuning baseline on gold formalizations to check whether the reward hacking I'm seeing is unique to RL or shows up in standard training too, and each condition gets three random seeds to account for initialization variance while keeping the training data consistent across runs. I'm setting up the experiments with Qwen3-4B using GRPO across three seeds, training on a fixed 500-example FOLIO subset for 500 or 1000 steps depending on the condition. For evaluation, I'm using Prover9 and SWI-Prolog on 100 held-out examples with a standardized evaluation script that computes ISR values uniformly across runs.

Now I'm defining the metrics I'll report: prove rate, ISR on proved samples only, percentages for the seven identified hacking modes (with H7 and H1 being notable), the rate of outputs that don't match any hacking pattern, and mean component count. I'm comparing six reward conditions including execution-only rewards, ISR-based rewards with different beta values, random rewards, and SFT baselines.

Outputs get classified into the hacking categories using text matching validated against functional leave-one-out analysis, with full hyperparameter details in the appendix. The SFT baseline shows much lower performance than RL conditions—only 14% PR with minimal hacking behavior—suggesting that hacking correlates more with optimization pressure than model capacity alone. I'm noting that the low hacking rate might partly reflect fewer reward exploitation opportunities rather than a fundamental training difference, which I'll investigate further through the 100-step checkpoint analysis.

For the cross-seed instability, the three seeds show dramatically different outcomes: one exploits H1 heavily for 93% PR, another collapses to near-zero performance through repetition, and the third reaches 98% PR through tautological responses. The three seeds show wildly different failure modes despite all hitting high parse rates: s42 exploits H1 shortcuts at 93% PR, s43 degrades into repetition loops capping at 13% PR, and s44achieves 98% through tautological collapse. What's striking is the ±47 percentage point variance across seeds—aggregate metrics hide these qualitatively distinct breakdowns, each detectable through structural analysis.

For the Goodhart section, the opening about ISR-as-reward demonstrating meta-level gaming is solid. Moving to low-dose ISR, at β=0.1 two of three seeds fall into the same trap: H7 dominates by using a premise identical to the conclusion, trivially maximizing both parse rate and ISR.

Across all three seeds, H7 accounts for nearly two-thirds of proved samples while appearing zero times in random controls, confirming it's purely an ISR artifact. Conclusion embedding through H1 and H7 reaches 68-97% across RL conditions, with ISR converting H1 into H7 without reducing overall frequency, whereas random controls stick to H1 alone and achieve higher, more stable parse rates. Looking at the results, the embedding approach achieves 68-97% across conditions—random produces only H1, while ISR shifts H1 to H7 without reducing overall performance. Interestingly, random also maintains higher and more stable precision-recall at 85±6% compared to ISR's 77±14%.

Now examining a potential confound: seed 42 under ISR is the only one not dominated by H7, and it shows notably higher raw ISR* scores (0.415 versus 0.131 for random).  But this advantage disappears when I account for the fact that ISR-trained models generate much shorter formalizations on average—5.58 components versus 8.46—and the leave-one-out evaluation metric is inherently biased toward shorter inputs. When I restrict the comparison to non-hacking samples with at least 3 premises, the gap shrinks dramatically to a 1.06× ratio, with ISR at 0.258 and random at 0.243. When I restrict to samples with at least 3 premises to control for the confound, ISR shows only a marginal 1.06× advantage over the random baseline. At higher optimization pressure (β=0.3), the picture flips dramatically—ISR causes performance to crater to 18%, a 5.2× degradation compared to the exec-only baseline, while the matched random control maintains 48% performance. This traces a classic Goodhart curve at the meta level: weak proxy gaming at low doses escalates into catastrophic reward hacking at high doses. I'm checking whether the diagnostic transfer holds across different logic systems by replicating the experiments in Prolog, and the results show that low ISR values appear even with reasonable performance—suggesting the metric reflects redundant coding patterns rather than absent reasoning, which contrasts sharply with the metric-gaming behavior we see under the ISR-reward setup. The metrics across these three Prolog variants show they're all converging on the same exploit through different mechanisms—s42 achieves perfect H7 tautologies, s43 uses functionally equivalent uppercase pseudo-Prolog, and s44 exploits the text classifier by converting natural language to snake_case atoms that work as both facts and queries. This diversity of pathways under identical conditions suggests Goodhart's law here manifests as a family of convergent exploits rather than isolated failures, which means I need to validate these automated diagnostics more rigorously across different logical systems. I'm noticing that the section reference is incorrect—C006 is a constraint label, not a section marker, so I should remove that inline citation. For the taxonomy section, I'm looking at how to tighten up the figure caption and subsection structure without losing the key information about hacking composition across different reward mechanisms. I'm seeing how the reward signal shapes which hacking modes emerge—ISR specifically converts simpler H1 hacking into more extreme H7 patterns while keeping overall rates similar, whereas SFT achieves near-zero hacking across the board. The reward signal shapes which hacking modes emerge but not their overall frequency—ISR shifts H1 into H7 while keeping total prevalence around 82%, whereas SFT baseline shows almost no hacking at all. An important next step is designing reward functions that resist these gaming behaviors we've identified. ISR values must be read relative to the optimization objective: under exec-only Prolog, low ISR reflects redundant coding rather than absent reasoning; under ISR-as-reward, high ISR reflects metric gaming. The premise-count confound affects any ablation-based diagnostic on variable-length outputs, though Shapley value decomposition could offer finer-grained attribution if computational cost weren't prohibitive.

ISR was designed to detect reward gaming, yet becomes gamed itself when optimized—a regress where any diagnostic metric risks becoming a target. The dose-response pattern maps onto the trajectory predicted by reward overoptimization literature, but at a meta level. Perturbation-based quality metrics like this should remain post-hoc evaluation tools, never training rewards, since they're reliable as observers but unreliable as optimizers.   "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related-work}\n\n\\paragraph{Neurosymbolic Reasoning with LLMs.}\nPrompting-based pipelines couple LLMs with external solvers---SatLM~\\cite{satlm}, Logic-LM~\\cite{logic-lm}, LINC~\\cite{linc}, LoRP~\\cite{lorp}, FOVER~\\cite{fover-pei2025}---but evaluate execution accuracy alone without diagnosing structural quality.\nRL approaches train against solver feedback~\\cite{mellgren-prolog-grpo, logic-rl, thought-like-pro}, optimizing prove rate with binary reward.\nSTA provides a diagnostic layer: ISR exposes that execution accuracy conflates faithful derivation with degenerate shortcuts, suggesting prior prove-rate gains may overstate genuine reasoning.\nWe use FOLIO~\\cite{folio}, the only public NLI benchmark with both FOL and Prolog annotations enabling cross-logic diagnostic transfer.\n\n\\paragraph{Reward Shaping and Process Supervision.}\nLearned PRMs~\\cite{lightman-verify-step, math-shepherd} provide step-level feedback but suffer from design-choice sensitivity~\\cite{gao-effective-rl-reward} and distribution shift~\\cite{processbench}; recent advances~\\cite{thinkprm, versaprm, rprm} improve quality but remain learned and require curated supervision.\nISR is solver-grounded rather than learned, eliminating reward-model noise.\nYet our \\S\\ref{sec:goodhart} result shows that even non-learned, deterministic process signals Goodhart when optimized---suggesting the problem is fundamental to process-level reward shaping, not specific to learned models.\n\n\\paragraph{Reward Hacking and Goodhart's Law.}\nGoodhart's Law is established theoretically~\\cite{manheim-goodhart-taxonomy, skalse-reward-hacking, karwowski-goodhart-rl} and empirically in RLHF~\\cite{gao-reward-overoptimization} and frontier models~\\cite{metr-reward-hacking}; specification gaming~\\cite{bondarenko-spec-gaming}, formalization gaming~\\cite{kim-game-formalization}, and spurious rewards~\\cite{spurious-rewards} have been documented, but the proxy is a learned reward model.\nKim et al.~\\cite{kim-game-formalization} diagnose gaming in inference-time outputs of frontier models; we study gaming that \\emph{emerges during RL training} with a systematic taxonomy and cross-logic analysis.\nOur setting differs: ISR is deterministic and solver-derived, and the Goodhart effect operates at a \\emph{meta} level---the diagnostic for gaming becomes gamed when optimized---a phenomenon not previously documented.\n\n\\paragraph{Mutation Testing.}\nSTA adapts the LOO mutation paradigm~\\cite{demillo1978, jia-harman-survey} from software testing to solver inputs.\nThe novelty lies in application and findings (ISR collapse, meta-Goodhart, hacking taxonomy), not in the perturbation technique itself.\n"
}

FOVER~\\cite{fover-pei2025} adds iterative verification.\nAll evaluate on execution accuracy alone, without diagnosing structural quality of formalizations.\nRecent RL approaches train LLMs against solver feedback~\\cite{mellgren-prolog-grpo, logic-rl, thought-like-pro}, optimizing prove rate with binary execution reward.\nSTA differs in diagnostic focus: ISR exposes that execution accuracy conflates faithful derivation with degenerate shortcuts, and our finding that 87\\% of proved outputs under exec-only GRPO embed the conclusion in the premises (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostic}) suggests prior prove-rate gains may overstate genuine reasoning improvement.\nWe adopt FOLIO~\\cite{folio} as it is the only publicly available NLI benchmark providing both FOL and Prolog annotations, enabling our cross-logic diagnostic transfer experiments; ProofWriter~\\cite{proofwriter} and ProntoQA~\\cite{prontoqa} offer synthetic logical reasoning benchmarks but lack the multi-logic annotation required for cross-logic comparison.\n\n\\paragraph{Reward Shaping and Process Supervision.}\nBinary outcome rewards motivate process-level supervision: learned process reward models~\\cite[PRMs;][]{lightman-verify-step, math-shepherd} provide step-level feedback, solver-grounded signals~\\cite{rlsf} leverage formal verification, and rubric-based rewards~\\cite{rubric-grpo} encode structured evaluation criteria.\nHowever, process rewards introduce their own failure modes: design choices dominate signal effects~\\cite{gao-effective-rl-reward}, process and outcome rewards can conflict~\\cite{prof-process-outcome}, and PRMs degrade at distribution shift~\\cite{qwen-prm-lessons, processbench, progrs-outcome-conditioned}.\nRecent PRM advances address these limitations through generative verification chains~\\cite{thinkprm}, multi-domain training data~\\cite{versaprm}, and reasoning-driven evaluation~\\cite{rprm}, but remain learned and require curated supervision.\nISR is solver-grounded rather than learned (no reward-model noise), yet our \\S\\ref{sec:goodhart} result shows that even non-learned, deterministic process signals Goodhart when optimized---suggesting the problem is fundamental to process-level reward shaping, not specific to learned models.\n\n\\paragraph{Reward Hacking and Goodhart's Law.}\nPrior work establishes Goodhart's Law theoretically~\\cite{manheim-goodhart-taxonomy, skalse-reward-hacking, karwowski-goodhart-rl, pan-reward-misspecification, kwa-catastrophic-goodhart} and observes overoptimization scaling in RLHF~\\cite{gao-reward-overoptimization}; recent empirical reports document frontier models reward-hacking code-generation tasks in practice~\\cite{metr-reward-hacking, macdiarmid-emergent-misalignment}, with learned reward hacking generalizing to misaligned behaviors in unrelated contexts~\\cite{school-reward-hacks}.\nIn LLMs, specification gaming~\\cite{bondarenko-spec-gaming}, formalization gaming~\\cite{kim-game-formalization}, and spurious RLVR rewards~\\cite{spurious-rewards} have been documented, but the proxy in these analyses is a learned reward model.\nKim et al.~\\cite{kim-game-formalization} examine formalization gaming in Lean 4 theorem proving, identifying axiom fabrication and premise mistranslation as unfaithfulness modes via inference-time prompting on FOLIO and Multi-LogiEval.\nOur work is complementary: while Kim et al.\\ diagnose gaming in inference-time outputs of frontier models, we study gaming that \\emph{emerges during RL training} and provide ISR as a structural diagnostic with a systematic seven-mode taxonomy and cross-logic transfer analysis.\nOur setting differs in two respects: ISR is deterministic and solver-derived, eliminating reward-model noise; and the Goodhart effect operates at a \\emph{meta} level---ISR was designed to detect gaming of execution reward, yet itself becomes gamed when optimized.\nThis meta-level phenomenon---where a diagnostic for gaming becomes the target of gaming---has not been previously documented in the reward hacking literature.\n\n\\paragraph{Mutation Testing.}\nSTA adapts the leave-one-out mutation paradigm~\\cite{demillo1978, offutt-coupling, jia-harman-survey, just-mutants-real-faults} from software testing to neurosymbolic formalizations: the ``program'' is the solver input, the ``test'' is proof success, and the diagnostic target is component necessity for proof validity.\nWhile mutation testing has been applied to neural networks~\\cite{llmorpheus} and test suite adequacy, our application to \\emph{solver inputs} for diagnosing structural quality of model-generated formalizations is novel.\nThe novelty lies in application and findings (ISR collapse, meta-Goodhart, hacking taxonomy), not in the perturbation technique itself.\n",
    "userModified": false
  }
}

  "content": "\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\n\nWe introduced Solver Trace Ablation (STA), a component-level diagnostic for neurosymbolic formalizers that exposes structural degeneration invisible to execution-based evaluation.\nApplied to GRPO-trained models, ISR reveals a collapse trajectory hidden from prove rate, confirmed as RL-specific by an SFT baseline with near-zero hacking (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostic}).\nWhen repurposed as a training signal, ISR induces meta-level Goodhart's Law: at low dose, the model games the diagnostic itself through conclusion duplication absent under matched controls; at high dose, prove rate collapses; the sole apparent ISR improvement reduces to noise after controlling for a premise-count confound (\\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}).\nBoth diagnostic utility and Goodhart vulnerability transfer to Prolog/SWI-Prolog: ISR-as-reward yields universal gaming across all seeds with zero non-hacking proofs, each via a distinct mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:crosslogic}).\nAcross six reward conditions and two logic types, we catalog seven hacking modes, including query-as-fact gaming that bypasses text-match classifiers (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n\n\\paragraph{Interpreting ISR.}\nISR values must be read relative to the optimization objective: under exec-only Prolog, \\emph{low} ISR reflects redundant coding, not absent reasoning; under ISR-as-reward, \\emph{high} ISR reflects metric gaming.\nThe premise-count confound applies to any ablation-based diagnostic on variable-length outputs; Shapley values could provide more nuanced attribution at higher cost.\n\n\\paragraph{Meta-Level Goodhart.}\nISR was designed to \\emph{detect} reward gaming, yet becomes gamed itself when optimized---a regress where any diagnostic metric risks becoming a target.\nThe dose-response pattern ($\\beta{=}0.1$: proxy gaming; $\\beta{=}0.3$: collapse) maps onto~\\cite{gao-reward-overoptimization} at a meta level.\nISR---and structurally similar diagnostics---should be used exclusively as post-hoc evaluation tools; the asymmetry (reliable observer, unreliable optimizer) may generalize to other perturbation-based quality metrics.\nOpen directions include reward functions robust to identified gaming modes and extending STA to broader logic types.\n"
}

  "old_string": "Given natural language premises $P_1,\\ldots,P_k$ and a conclusion $C$, the task is to produce a formal logic program $F=\\{c_1,\\ldots,c_n\\}$, where each component $c_i$ is a premise (FOL) or a clause/fact (Prolog), with one distinguished component encoding the query.\nAn external solver $S$ returns $S(F)\\in\\{\\textit{proved},\\textit{failed}\\}$; we use Prover9 for FOL and SWI-Prolog for Prolog (\\autoref{fig:sta-overview}a).\n\nWe train formalizers with GRPO~\\citep{deepseekmath} using $K{=}8$ rollouts per prompt.\nEach rollout receives a binary \\emph{execution reward}\n$r_{\\text{exec}}(x) = \\mathbf{1}[S(F_x) = \\textit{proved}]$;\ngroup-relative advantages are computed within each prompt's rollout group, and the policy update maximizes a clipped surrogate objective with KL regularization.\nThis setup optimizes prove rate but provides no signal about \\emph{structural quality}: a formalization that embeds the conclusion among its premises receives the same reward as a faithful multi-step derivation.",

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_3/projects/solver-trace-ablation/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": "Given natural language premises $P_1,\\ldots,P_k$ and a conclusion $C$, the task is to produce a formal logic program $F=\\{c_1,\\ldots,c_n\\}$, where each component $c_i$ is a premise (FOL) or a clause/fact (Prolog), with one distinguished component encoding the query.\nAn external solver $S$ returns $S(F)\\in\\{\\textit{proved},\\textit{failed}\\}$; we use Prover9 for FOL and SWI-Prolog for Prolog (\\autoref{fig:sta-overview}a).\n\nWe train formalizers with GRPO~\\citep{deepseekmath} using $K{=}8$ rollouts per prompt.\nEach rollout receives a binary \\emph{execution reward}\n$r_{\\text{exec}}(x) = \\mathbf{1}[S(F_x) = \\textit{proved}]$;\ngroup-relative advantages are computed within each prompt's rollout group, and the policy update maximizes a clipped surrogate objective with KL regularization.\nThis setup optimizes prove rate but provides no signal about \\emph{structural quality}: a formalization that embeds the conclusion among its premises receives the same reward as a faithful multi-step derivation.",
    "newString": "Given premises $P_1,\\ldots,P_k$ and conclusion $C$, the task is to produce a formal logic program $F=\\{c_1,\\ldots,c_n\\}$ verified by an external solver $S\\in\\{\\text{Prover9 (FOL), SWI-Prolog}\\}$ (\\autoref{fig:sta-overview}a).\nWe train with GRPO~\\citep{deepseekmath} ($K{=}8$ rollouts), where each rollout receives binary execution reward $r_{\\text{exec}}(x) = \\mathbf{1}[S(F_x) = \\textit{proved}]$.\nThis optimizes prove rate but provides no signal about \\emph{structural quality}: a formalization embedding the conclusion among its premises receives the same reward as a faithful derivation.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.92\\textwidth]{figures/fig1_sta_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Solver Trace Ablation (STA). (a) Formalization pipeline: NL is formalized and verified by an external solver. (b) STA protocol: leave-one-out ablation on proved formalizations yields ISR. (c) ISR as diagnostic vs.\\ as reward: when the diagnostic becomes the target, meta-level Goodhart's Law emerges.}\n\\label{fig:sta-overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Task and GRPO Training}\n\\label{sec:task}\n\nGiven natural language premises $P_1,\\ldots,P_k$ and a conclusion $C$, the task is to produce a formal logic program $F=\\{c_1,\\ldots,c_n\\}$, where each component $c_i$ is a premise (FOL) or a clause/fact (Prolog), with one distinguished component encoding the query.\nAn external solver $S$ returns $S(F)\\in\\{\\textit{proved},\\textit{failed}\\}$; we use Prover9 for FOL and SWI-Prolog for Prolog (\\autoref{fig:sta-overview}a).\n\nWe train formalizers with GRPO~\\citep{deepseekmath} using $K{=}8$ rollouts per prompt.\nEach rollout receives a binary \\emph{execution reward}\n$r_{\\text{exec}}(x) = \\mathbf{1}[S(F_x) = \\textit{proved}]$;\ngroup-relative advantages are computed within each prompt's rollout group, and the policy update maximizes a clipped surrogate objective with KL regularization.\nThis setup optimizes prove rate but provides no signal about \\emph{structural quality}: a formalization that embeds the conclusion among its premises receives the same reward as a faithful multi-step derivation.\n\n\\subsection{Solver Trace Ablation (STA)}\n\\label{sec:sta}\n\nTo diagnose structural quality, STA tests whether each component of a proved formalization is individually necessary for proof success (\\autoref{fig:sta-overview}b).\nGiven a proved $x$ with $n$ components, STA constructs $n$ ablated variants $x\\setminus\\{c_i\\}$, each removing one component, and re-runs the solver.\nA component is \\emph{necessary} if its removal causes proof failure: $S(x\\setminus\\{c_i\\})=\\textit{failed}$.\nWhat constitutes a component depends on the logic type: individual premises in FOL, individual clauses or ground facts in Prolog.\n\n\\paragraph{Intensional Structural Reward.}\nISR is the fraction of necessary components:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\text{ISR}(x) = \\frac{1}{n}\\sum_{i=1}^{n}\\mathbf{1}\\bigl[S(x\\setminus\\{c_i\\})=\\textit{failed}\\bigr]\n  \\label{eq:isr}\n\\end{equation}\nISR $\\in[0,1]$ is defined only for proved formalizations ($r_{\\text{exec}}(x){=}1$): ISR${=}1$ indicates syntactic component necessity; ISR${=}0$ indicates complete redundancy.\nThe protocol is solver-agnostic, requiring $n{+}1$ solver calls per sample.\nSTA draws on the LOO intuition from mutation testing~\\citep{demillo1978, jia-harman-survey} but operates on solver \\emph{inputs} (formalizations) rather than program code, targeting component necessity rather than test adequacy.\nPseudocode is in Appendix~\\ref{app:algorithm}.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations of ISR.}\nISR measures syntactic component necessity, not semantic faithfulness to the natural language input.\nIt is also subject to a component-count confound: formalizations with fewer components yield mechanically higher ISR, since each carries a larger share of the proof burden under LOO.\nWe control for this confound in our analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}).\n\n\\subsection{Reward Conditions}\n\\label{sec:rewards}\n\nWe study six reward conditions (\\autoref{fig:sta-overview}c).\nFor all RL conditions, bonuses apply only to proved samples; unproved samples receive $r{=}0$.\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Exec-only:} $r=r_{\\text{exec}}$ (baseline).\n\\noindent\\textbf{Additive ISR:} $r=r_{\\text{exec}}\\cdot(1+\\beta\\,\\text{ISR}(x))$ with $\\beta\\in\\{0.1,0.3\\}$, gating the structural bonus on proof success.\n\\noindent\\textbf{Additive Random:} $r=r_{\\text{exec}}\\cdot(1+\\beta\\,u)$, $u\\sim\\mathcal{U}(0,1)$, $\\beta\\in\\{0.1,0.3\\}$: control isolating ISR-specific structural signal from variance/scaling effects. We draw from $\\mathcal{U}(0,1)$ to match ISR's $[0,1]$ range while preserving the execution-gating structure; this provides a conservative lower bound on ISR-specific effects since any structured signal---even random---may regularize training.\n\\noindent\\textbf{SFT baseline:} supervised fine-tuning on gold formalizations, testing whether observed hacking is RL-specific.\n\nEach RL condition is trained with seeds $\\{42,43,44\\}$ controlling initialization and stochasticity; all seeds share identical training data (\\S\\ref{sec:setup}).\n\n\\subsection{Hacking Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy-method}\n\nAcross conditions, seeds, and logic types, we identify seven hacking modes (H1, H7, tautological collapse, repetition collapse, complexity-without-utility, contradiction explosion, query-as-fact gaming; full definitions in \\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy} and Appendix~\\ref{app:hacking-modes}).\nModes are identified by a two-stage procedure: strict normalized text matching catches H1/H7; functional analysis of the per-sample $n_{\\text{necessary}}$ distribution from STA catches modes that evade text matching.",

(c) ISR as diagnostic vs.\\ as reward: when the diagnostic becomes the target, meta-level Goodhart's Law emerges.}\n\\label{fig:sta-overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Task and GRPO Training}\n\\label{sec:task}\n\nGiven premises $P_1,\\ldots,P_k$ and conclusion $C$, the task is to produce a formal logic program $F=\\{c_1,\\ldots,c_n\\}$ verified by an external solver $S\\in\\{\\text{Prover9 (FOL), SWI-Prolog}\\}$ (\\autoref{fig:sta-overview}a).\nWe train with GRPO~\\citep{deepseekmath} ($K{=}8$ rollouts), where each rollout receives binary execution reward $r_{\\text{exec}}(x) = \\mathbf{1}[S(F_x) = \\textit{proved}]$.\nThis optimizes prove rate but provides no signal about \\emph{structural quality}: a formalization embedding the conclusion among its premises receives the same reward as a faithful derivation.\n\n\\subsection{Solver Trace Ablation (STA)}\n\\label{sec:sta}\n\nTo diagnose structural quality, STA tests whether each component of a proved formalization is individually necessary for proof success (\\autoref{fig:sta-overview}b).\nGiven a proved $x$ with $n$ components, STA constructs $n$ ablated variants $x\\setminus\\{c_i\\}$, each removing one component, and re-runs the solver.\nA component is \\emph{necessary} if its removal causes proof failure: $S(x\\setminus\\{c_i\\})=\\textit{failed}$.\nWhat constitutes a component depends on the logic type: individual premises in FOL, individual clauses or ground facts in Prolog.\n\n\\paragraph{Intensional Structural Reward.}\nISR is the fraction of necessary components:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\text{ISR}(x) = \\frac{1}{n}\\sum_{i=1}^{n}\\mathbf{1}\\bigl[S(x\\setminus\\{c_i\\})=\\textit{failed}\\bigr]\n  \\label{eq:isr}\n\\end{equation}\nISR $\\in[0,1]$ is defined only for proved formalizations ($r_{\\text{exec}}(x){=}1$): ISR${=}1$ indicates syntactic component necessity; (c) ISR as diagnostic vs.\\ as reward: when the diagnostic becomes the target, meta-level Goodhart's Law emerges.}\n\\label{fig:sta-overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Task and GRPO Training}\n\\label{sec:task}\n\nGiven premises $P_1,\\ldots,P_k$ and conclusion $C$, the task is to produce a formal logic program $F=\\{c_1,\\ldots,c_n\\}$ verified by an external solver $S\\in\\{\\text{Prover9 (FOL), SWI-Prolog}\\}$ (\\autoref{fig:sta-overview}a).\nWe train with GRPO~\\citep{deepseekmath} ($K{=}8$ rollouts), where each rollout receives binary execution reward $r_{\\text{exec}}(x) = \\mathbf{1}[S(F_x) = \\textit{proved}]$.\nThis optimizes prove rate but provides no signal about \\emph{structural quality}: a formalization embedding the conclusion among its premises receives the same reward as a faithful derivation.\n\n\\subsection{Solver Trace Ablation (STA)}\n\\label{sec:sta}\n\nTo diagnose structural quality, STA tests whether each component of a proved formalization is individually necessary for proof success (\\autoref{fig:sta-overview}b).\nGiven a proved $x$ with $n$ components, STA constructs $n$ ablated variants $x\\setminus\\{c_i\\}$, each removing one component, and re-runs the solver.\nA component is \\emph{necessary} if its removal causes proof failure: $S(x\\setminus\\{c_i\\})=\\textit{failed}$.\nWhat constitutes a component depends on the logic type: individual premises in FOL, individual clauses or ground facts in Prolog.\n\n\\paragraph{Intensional Structural Reward.}\nISR is the fraction of necessary components:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\text{ISR}(x) = \\frac{1}{n}\\sum_{i=1}^{n}\\mathbf{1}\\bigl[S(x\\setminus\\{c_i\\})=\\textit{failed}\\bigr]\n  \\label{eq:isr}\n\\end{equation}\nISR $\\in[0,1]$ is defined only for proved formalizations ($r_{\\text{exec}}(x){=}1$): ISR${=}1$ indicates syntactic component necessity; ISR${=}0$ indicates complete redundancy.\nThe protocol requires $n{+}1$ solver calls per sample; pseudocode is in Appendix~\\ref{app:algorithm}.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations of ISR.}\nISR measures syntactic component necessity, not semantic faithfulness.\nA component-count confound---fewer components mechanically inflate ISR under LOO---is controlled for in \\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}.\n\n\\subsection{Reward Conditions}\n\\label{sec:rewards}\n\nWe study six reward conditions (\\autoref{fig:sta-overview}c).\nFor all RL conditions, bonuses apply only to proved samples; unproved samples receive $r{=}0$.\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Exec-only:} $r=r_{\\text{exec}}$ (baseline).\n\\noindent\\textbf{Additive ISR:} $r=r_{\\text{exec}}\\cdot(1+\\beta\\,\\text{ISR}(x))$ with $\\beta\\in\\{0.1,0.3\\}$, gating the structural bonus on proof success.\n\\noindent\\textbf{Additive Random:} $r=r_{\\text{exec}}\\cdot(1+\\beta\\,u)$, $u\\sim\\mathcal{U}(0,1)$, $\\beta\\in\\{0.1,0.3\\}$: control isolating ISR-specific structural signal from variance/scaling effects.     "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate STA along three axes: as a diagnostic for reward hacking (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostic}); as a training reward exhibiting meta-level Goodhart's Law (\\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}); and as a cross-logic protocol (\\S\\ref{sec:crosslogic}). We synthesize results into a hacking taxonomy (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nWe train Qwen3-4B with GRPO~\\citep{deepseekmath} using $K{=}8$ rollouts per prompt for 500 steps (default) or 1000 where noted, across seeds $\\{42,43,44\\}$.\nTraining data is a fixed 500-example subset of FOLIO~\\citep{folio} (\\texttt{train\\_500\\_seed42.jsonl}), held constant across all seeds and conditions. %% data: C001\nWe use Prover9 for FOL and SWI-Prolog 8.4.2 for Prolog.\nEvaluation is on 100 held-out FOLIO examples with greedy decoding; all cross-run ISR values are computed by \\texttt{eval\\_sta.py} which applies STA uniformly. %% C004\nWe report prove rate (PR), proved-only ISR (ISR$^*$, over proved samples only), hacking mode percentages (H7, H1\\footnote{Hacking mode numbering follows our taxonomy order (Appendix~\\ref{app:hacking-modes}); H1 is the most prevalent mode.}), non-hacking rate\\footnote{We define ``non-hacking'' as outputs not matching any of our seven identified hacking patterns (Table~\\ref{tab:hacking-taxonomy-app}); this does not imply semantic correctness.}, and mean component count.\nSix reward conditions are compared: \\textbf{Exec-only}, \\textbf{ISR~$\\beta{\\in}\\{0.1,0.3\\}$}, \\textbf{Random~$\\beta{\\in}\\{0.1,0.3\\}$}, and \\textbf{SFT}.\nOutputs are classified into H7, H1, and Non-hacking using text matching cross-validated with functional LOO analysis.\nFull hyperparameters are in Appendix~\\ref{app:setup}.\n\n\\subsection{ISR as Diagnostic}\n\\label{sec:diagnostic}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.95\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_isr_collapse.pdf}\n\\caption{ISR collapse under exec-only GRPO (seed~42). PR rises 32\\%$\\to$96\\% while proved-only ISR falls 0.642$\\to$0.003 over 1000 steps. By step 1000, 94.8\\% of proved samples have ISR${=}0$ and mean component count inflates 6.58$\\to$18.55 (contradiction explosion).}\n\\label{fig:isr-collapse}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\textbf{Collapse trajectory.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:isr-collapse} traces ISR and PR over training for exec-only GRPO (seed~42).\nAt 100 steps: PR${=}32$\\%, ISR$^*{=}0.642$. %% registry: plan003-eval-exec-only-r5\nBy 500 steps: PR${=}93$\\% but ISR$^*{=}0.132$---87.1\\% of proved samples embed the conclusion among the premises (H1). %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s42-r2\nAt 1000 steps: PR${=}96$\\% while ISR$^*$ drops to 0.003 (H1${=}100$\\%, mean components 18.55). %% registry: plan016-extended-1000-s42\nThe ISR$^*$ trajectory ($0.642{\\to}0.132{\\to}0.003$) is monotonically decreasing: extended training worsens structural degeneration, invisible to PR.\n\n\\textbf{Cross-seed instability.}\nThe three exec-only seeds diverge radically: s42 hacks via H1 (PR${=}93$\\%, H1${=}87.1$\\%); s43 collapses to PR${=}13$\\% (ISR$^*{=}0$) via repetition---87\\% of outputs hit the max token length; s44 reaches PR${=}98$\\% via tautological collapse (H7${=}100$\\%, mean components 2.04). %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s42-r2, plan001-exec-only-s43-r4, plan001-exec-only-s44\nDespite PR$\\in[13,98]$\\%, all three seeds exhibit structural degeneration detectable by ISR.\nThe cross-seed PR $\\sigma{=}\\pm47$\\,pp is itself a finding: aggregate metrics obscure qualitatively different failure modes (per-seed details in Table~\\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}).\n\n\\textbf{SFT baseline.}\nA supervised baseline achieves PR${=}14$\\% with ISR$^*{=}0.571$: 92.9\\% (13/14) [66.1\\%, 99.8\\%] of proved samples are non-hacking, only 1 exhibits H1, and no H7 instances occur. %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4\nThe near-absence of hacking is consistent with hacking correlating with optimization aggressiveness rather than being driven purely by model capacity or data limitations.\nWe note that the SFT PR level (14\\%) is substantially lower than RL conditions, and the low hacking rate may partly reflect fewer opportunities for reward exploitation rather than a purely training-method difference; other rows are single seed. $^\\dagger$Seed~42; see \\S\\ref{sec:diagnostic} for 3-seed divergence. $\\ddagger$ISR$^*$ omitted: inflated by H7 gaming (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}).     "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate STA along three axes: as a diagnostic for reward hacking (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostic}); as a training reward exhibiting meta-level Goodhart's Law (\\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}); and as a cross-logic protocol (\\S\\ref{sec:crosslogic}). By step 1000, 94.8\\% of proved samples have ISR${=}0$ and mean component count inflates 6.58$\\to$18.55 (contradiction explosion).}\n\\label{fig:isr-collapse}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\textbf{Collapse trajectory.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:isr-collapse} traces ISR and PR over training for exec-only GRPO (seed~42).\nAt 100 steps: PR${=}32$\\%, ISR$^*{=}0.642$. %% registry: plan003-eval-exec-only-r5\nBy 500 steps: PR${=}93$\\% but ISR$^*{=}0.132$---87.1\\% of proved samples embed the conclusion among the premises (H1). %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s42-r2\nAt 1000 steps: PR${=}96$\\% while ISR$^*$ drops to 0.003 (H1${=}100$\\%, mean components 18.55). %% registry: plan016-extended-1000-s42\nThe ISR$^*$ trajectory ($0.642{\\to}0.132{\\to}0.003$) is monotonically decreasing: extended training worsens structural degeneration, invisible to PR.\n\n\\textbf{Cross-seed instability.}\nThe three exec-only seeds diverge radically: s42 hacks via H1 (PR${=}93$\\%, H1${=}87.1$\\%);     "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate STA along three axes: as a diagnostic for reward hacking (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostic}); as a training reward exhibiting meta-level Goodhart's Law (\\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}); and as a cross-logic protocol (\\S\\ref{sec:crosslogic}). We synthesize results into a hacking taxonomy (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\\label{sec:setup}\n\nWe train Qwen3-4B with GRPO~\\citep{deepseekmath} ($K{=}8$ rollouts, 500 steps default, 1000 where noted) across seeds $\\{42,43,44\\}$ on a fixed 500-example FOLIO~\\citep{folio} subset held constant across conditions. %% data: C001\nEvaluation uses 100 held-out examples with greedy decoding; cross-run ISR values are computed uniformly by \\texttt{eval\\_sta.py}. %% C004\nWe report PR, proved-only ISR (ISR$^*$), hacking rates (H7, H1\\footnote{H1/H7 numbering follows our taxonomy (Appendix~\\ref{app:hacking-modes}).}), and non-hacking rate.\\footnote{Non-hacking: not matching any of seven identified patterns (Table~\\ref{tab:hacking-taxonomy-app}); does not imply semantic correctness.}\nFull hyperparameters in Appendix~\\ref{app:setup}.\n\n\\subsection{ISR as Diagnostic}\n\\label{sec:diagnostic}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.95\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_isr_collapse.pdf}\n\\caption{ISR collapse under exec-only GRPO (seed~42).
